Well, yes, I suppose it's mostly just those kinds of places. I mean... I know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up somewhere terrible. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me. I hope I'm not wasting your time. It's just a small thing, really, nothing too terrible. But... well, whenever I step into one of those slow elevators, or, you know, a small room where the door is locked... my chest just jumps right away. \n\nI get this really strong need to get right back out before I can even think straight. It's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, to be honest. I try not to make a fuss about it, but... well, I hope that's the sort of thing I'm supposed to talk about here. Am I explaining that alright for you?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of needing to get out limited to enclosed or small spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it's mostly just those kinds of places. I mean... I know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up somewhere terrible. It's perfectly safe, I know that. But... my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? I know I shouldn't react that way, and I really do try to just push through it so I don't bother anyone. But it's just those closed-in spots without a clear way out. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding situations or places that might trigger these feelings of needing to get out?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I do, a little bit. It's nothing major, really, just small adjustments so I don't hold anyone up or make a scene. Like... well, these days, I just take the stairs instead of the elevator. It's good exercise for a fellow my age anyway, right? \n\nAnd down at the museum where I volunteer, I usually pass on the archive jobs in the basement. Or, if I absolutely have to go into a tight spot and there's no way around it, I might... well, I might ask a friend to just wait nearby. Just in case. \n\nI know it sounds a bit foolish, a grown man needing a buddy to check a storage closet. I try not to be a bother about it, honestly. Does that sound like the sort of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, in these situations?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing quite that dramatic, I don't think. I don't fall to pieces. I hope that's okay? I know you have to ask these things to figure out what's going on, so I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers. Am I being helpful at all?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or discomfort limited to being in enclosed spaces, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really think it's just the small spaces, honestly. I don't get that... that feeling anywhere else. \n\nI mean, I can be out in a crowd, or up high somewhere, or... or even flying on an airplane, and I'm right as rain. It's just those little windowless rooms and elevators that seem to bother me. \n\nI hope that's the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you all the pieces you need. I really don't think there's anything else that sets it off, but if you want me to think on it more, I certainly can.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being trapped or the small size of the space itself that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more about feeling trapped. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, you know? It's perfectly safe. But... well, my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? A man my age getting worked up over a door closing. I try to just brush it off so it doesn't bother anyone. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it right so it helps you figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that something terrible will happen to you when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't think I'm going to be hurt or anything like that. It's not like I'm worried the ceiling is going to cave in on me or the elevator cables will snap. \n\nIt's just that uncomfortable feeling, you know? Just wanting to be back out where there's a bit more room to breathe. I certainly don't let my imagination run away with me about terrible accidents or anything. I try to keep a level head about it. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I mean, I figure if I don't have those awful thoughts, then maybe it's not such a big problem after all. Does that sound right to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack or some other intense reaction when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I don't think it's anything like that. A panic attack sounds so... well, so severe, doesn't it? I certainly don't go having those. I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I'd hate to be a bother to anyone, so I just try to be a good sport and push through it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I... I'd never do anything like that. Losing control, I mean. That would be terribly embarrassing, wouldn't it? \n\nI just get a little eager to step back out, that's all. I certainly don't feel like my body is going to do anything frightening or... or strange. I've always prided myself on keeping a stiff upper lip, you know. I just stand quietly and mind my own business until I can leave. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm the sort of fellow who goes to pieces over a small room? I try very hard to be sensible about it, Doctor, and just behave myself. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for? Well, it's... it's just the spaces themselves, I suppose. It's not that I think there's a monster in the closet or anything silly like that. 

I know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being... well, locked up. But my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's just that feeling of being shut in, you know?

I suppose I do, a little bit. It's nothing major, really, just small adjustments so I don't hold anyone up or make a scene. Like... well, these days, I just take the stairs instead of the elevator. It's good exercise for a fellow my age anyway, right? \n\nAnd down at the museum where I volunteer, I usually pass on the archive jobs in the basement. Or, if I absolutely have to go into a tight spot and there's no way around it, I might... well, I might ask a friend to just wait nearby. Just in case. \n\nI know it sounds a bit foolish, a grown man needing a buddy to check a storage closet. I try not to be a bother about it, honestly. Does that sound like the sort of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, in these situations?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing quite that dramatic, I don't think. I mean... my heart might beat a little bit quicker just because I'm eager to step back out, but it doesn't race, and I certainly don't lose my breath over it. \n\nIt's really just a strong urge to turn around and leave, not a... well, not a big episode or anything. I don't fall to pieces. I hope that's okay? I know you have to ask these things to figure out what's going on, so I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers. Am I being helpful at all?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or discomfort limited to being in enclosed spaces, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really think it's just the small spaces, honestly. I don't get that... that feeling anywhere else. \n\nI mean, I can be out in a crowd, or up high somewhere, or... or even flying on an airplane, and I'm right as rain. It's just those little windowless rooms and elevators that seem to bother me. \n\nI hope that's the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you all the pieces you need. I really don't think there's anything else that sets it off, but if you want me to think on it more, I certainly can.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being trapped or the small size of the space itself that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more about feeling trapped. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, you know? It's perfectly safe. But... well, my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? A man my age getting worked up over a door closing. I try to just brush it off so it doesn't bother anyone. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it right so it helps you figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that something terrible will happen to you when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Well, I... I suppose you could say that, yes. It's just that feeling of the door being closed and not being able to just step right back out if I wanted to.

It's good exercise for a fellow my age anyway, right? \n\nAnd down at the museum where I volunteer, I usually pass on the archive jobs in the basement. Or, if I absolutely have to go into a tight spot and there's no way around it, I might... well, I might ask a friend to just wait nearby. Just in case. \n\nI know it sounds a bit foolish, a grown man needing a buddy to check a storage closet. I try not to be a bother about it, honestly. Does that sound like the sort of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, in these situations?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing quite that dramatic, I don't think. I mean... my heart might beat a little bit quicker just because I'm eager to step back out, but it doesn't race, and I certainly don't lose my breath over it. \n\nIt's really just a strong urge to turn around and leave, not a... well, not a big episode or anything. I don't fall to pieces. I hope that's okay? I know you have to ask these things to figure out what's going on, so I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers. Am I being helpful at all?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or discomfort limited to being in enclosed spaces, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really think it's just the small spaces, honestly. I don't get that... that feeling anywhere else. \n\nI mean, I can be out in a crowd, or up high somewhere, or... or even flying on an airplane, and I'm right as rain. It's just those little windowless rooms and elevators that seem to bother me. \n\nI hope that's the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you all the pieces you need. I really don't think there's anything else that sets it off, but if you want me to think on it more, I certainly can.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being trapped or the small size of the space itself that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more about feeling trapped. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, you know? It's perfectly safe. But... well, my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? A man my age getting worked up over a door closing. I try to just brush it off so it doesn't bother anyone. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it right so it helps you figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that something terrible will happen to you when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't think I'm going to be hurt or anything like that. It's not like I'm worried the ceiling is going to cave in on me or the elevator cables will snap. \n\nIt's just that uncomfortable feeling, you know? Just wanting to be back out where there's a bit more room to breathe. I certainly don't let my imagination run away with me about terrible accidents or anything. I try to keep a level head about it. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I mean, I figure if I don't have those awful thoughts, then maybe it's not such a big problem after all. Does that sound right to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack or some other intense reaction when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. It's a bit embarrassing to admit at my age, to be honest. I try not to make a fuss about it, but... well, I hope that's the sort of thing I'm supposed to talk about here. Am I explaining that alright for you?\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of needing to get out limited to enclosed or small spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it's mostly just those kinds of places. I mean... I know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up somewhere terrible. It's perfectly safe, I know that. But... my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? I know I shouldn't react that way, and I really do try to just push through it so I don't bother anyone. But it's just those closed-in spots without a clear way out. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding situations or places that might trigger these feelings of needing to get out?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose I do, a little bit. It's nothing major, really, just small adjustments so I don't hold anyone up or make a scene. Like... well, these days, I just take the stairs instead of the elevator. It's good exercise for a fellow my age anyway, right? \n\nAnd down at the museum where I volunteer, I usually pass on the archive jobs in the basement. Or, if I absolutely have to go into a tight spot and there's no way around it, I might... well, I might ask a friend to just wait nearby. Just in case. \n\nI know it sounds a bit foolish, a grown man needing a buddy to check a storage closet. I try not to be a bother about it, honestly. Does that sound like the sort of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, in these situations?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing quite that dramatic, I don't think. I mean... my heart might beat a little bit quicker just because I'm eager to step back out, but it doesn't race, and I certainly don't lose my breath over it. \n\nIt's really just a strong urge to turn around and leave, not a... well, not a big episode or anything. I don't fall to pieces. I hope that's okay? I know you have to ask these things to figure out what's going on, so I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers. Am I being helpful at all?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or discomfort limited to being in enclosed spaces, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really think it's just the small spaces, honestly. I don't get that... that feeling anywhere else. \n\nI mean, I can be out in a crowd, or up high somewhere, or... or even flying on an airplane, and I'm right as rain. It's just those little windowless rooms and elevators that seem to bother me. \n\nI hope that's the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you all the pieces you need. I really don't think there's anything else that sets it off, but if you want me to think on it more, I certainly can.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being trapped or the small size of the space itself that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more about feeling trapped. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, you know? It's perfectly safe. But... well, my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? A man my age getting worked up over a door closing. I try to just brush it off so it doesn't bother anyone. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it right so it helps you figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that something terrible will happen to you when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't think I'm going to be hurt or anything like that. It's not like I'm worried the ceiling is going to cave in on me or the elevator cables will snap. \n\nIt's just that uncomfortable feeling, you know? Just wanting to be back out where there's a bit more room to breathe. I certainly don't let my imagination run away with me about terrible accidents or anything. I try to keep a level head about it. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I mean, I figure if I don't have those awful thoughts, then maybe it's not such a big problem after all. Does that sound right to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack or some other intense reaction when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I don't think it's anything like that. A panic attack sounds so... well, so severe, doesn't it? I certainly don't go having those. I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I'd hate to be a bother to anyone, so I just try to be a good sport and push through it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I... I'd never do anything like that. Losing control, I mean. That would be terribly embarrassing, wouldn't it? \n\nI just get a little eager to step back out, that's all. I certainly don't feel like my body is going to do anything frightening or... or strange. I've always prided myself on keeping a stiff upper lip, you know. I just stand quietly and mind my own business until I can leave. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm the sort of fellow who goes to pieces over a small room? I try very hard to be sensible about it, Doctor, and just behave myself. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in enclosed spaces, or is there something specific about those spaces that frightens you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's just the spaces themselves, I suppose. It's not that I think there's a monster in the closet or anything silly like that. \n\nI know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being... well, locked up. But my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's just that feeling of being shut in, you know? \n\nI try to remind myself that it's just a room and I'm perfectly safe. I'm not being foolish about it, am I? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it properly so I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Is it the lack of an easy way to exit that bothers you when you're in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose you could say that, yes. It's just that feeling of the door being closed and not being able to just step right back out if I wanted to. \n\nIt's not that I think I'm trapped forever, of course. I know perfectly well that people come and go from these places all day long without a hitch. I just prefer to have a clear path to the door, that's all. \n\nI try to just stand near the exit and not make a fuss about it. I don't want to seem like an old fool who can't handle a simple elevator ride. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear that being in an enclosed space will trigger some kind of intense anxiety episode?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. Nothing like that. I don't have... episodes or anything dramatic. I'm not the type to just fly off the handle or make a scene. \n\nIt's just a mild discomfort, really. A little flutter, maybe, but I just keep it to myself. I certainly wouldn't call it intense anxiety. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more the space itself. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a cell or anything dreadful like that. 

But my mind just... well, it treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's silly, isn't it? I know perfectly well I'm safe, but I just get this little urge to step back out where there's more room to breathe. 

I try not to make a fuss, of course. I just politely take the stairs if I can, or wait by the door. I hope that makes sense, Doctor. I'm not trying to be difficult, I just want to explain it right so you have what you need. Is that helpful?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I suppose it's not so much about being trapped, exactly. I mean, I know the door isn't locked, usually. It's not like I think I'm a prisoner or anything silly like that. 

It's really just the feeling of the walls being too close, you know? Just the... the smallness of it all. I know I could push the door open or press the button if I really had to, so it's not that I think I can't escape. I just... I just don't like the tight squeeze. 

I hope I'm not repeating myself too much. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea about it all. Does that answer your question, Doctor?

I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more about feeling trapped. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, you know? It's perfectly safe. But... well, my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? A man my age getting worked up over a door closing. I try to just brush it off so it doesn't bother anyone. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it right so it helps you figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that something terrible will happen to you when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Does that sound right to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack or some other intense reaction when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I don't think it's anything like that. A panic attack sounds so... well, so severe, doesn't it? I certainly don't go having those. I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I'd hate to be a bother to anyone, so I just try to be a good sport and push through it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. Does that sound right to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack or some other intense reaction when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I don't think it's anything like that. A panic attack sounds so... well, so severe, doesn't it? I certainly don't go having those. I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I'd hate to be a bother to anyone, so I just try to be a good sport and push through it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I... I'd never do anything like that. Losing control, I mean. That would be terribly embarrassing, wouldn't it? \n\nI just get a little eager to step back out, that's all. I certainly don't feel like my body is going to do anything frightening or... or strange. I've always prided myself on keeping a stiff upper lip, you know. I just stand quietly and mind my own business until I can leave. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm the sort of fellow who goes to pieces over a small room? I try very hard to be sensible about it, Doctor, and just behave myself. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in enclosed spaces, or is there something specific about those spaces that frightens you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's just the spaces themselves, I suppose. It's not that I think there's a monster in the closet or anything silly like that. \n\nI know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being... well, locked up. But my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's just that feeling of being shut in, you know? \n\nI try to remind myself that it's just a room and I'm perfectly safe. I'm not being foolish about it, am I? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it properly so I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Is it the lack of an easy way to exit that bothers you when you're in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose you could say that, yes. It's just that feeling of the door being closed and not being able to just step right back out if I wanted to. \n\nIt's not that I think I'm trapped forever, of course. I know perfectly well that people come and go from these places all day long without a hitch. I just prefer to have a clear path to the door, that's all. \n\nI try to just stand near the exit and not make a fuss about it. I don't want to seem like an old fool who can't handle a simple elevator ride. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear that being in an enclosed space will trigger some kind of intense anxiety episode?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. Nothing like that. I don't have... episodes or anything dramatic. I'm not the type to just fly off the handle or make a scene. \n\nIt's just a mild discomfort, really. A little flutter, maybe, but I just keep it to myself. I certainly wouldn't call it intense anxiety. I've seen people get all worked up over things, and I always think it's better to just keep your composure, don't you agree? \n\nI just try to be a good sport about it. I hope you don't think I'm one of those people who can't control their nerves. I'm doing my best to be helpful and give you the right answers, Doctor. Am I doing alright?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only about being in small or enclosed spaces, without any other specific concerns?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I believe so. It's really just the small spaces. I don't have any other peculiar worries or... or odd habits, if that's what you mean. \n\nI'm not afraid of heights or spiders or anything like that. I try to be very sensible about most things, you know? It's really just that one little quirk about being cooped up in a tight spot, and even then, I manage it as best I can. \n\nI hope that helps narrow things down for you. I'm trying to be as clear as I can so I don't confuse matters... is that the sort of answer you were looking for, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Is it the small size of the space itself that bothers you, or is it more about not being able to leave when you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more the space itself. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a cell or anything dreadful like that. \n\nBut my mind just... well, it treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's silly, isn't it? I know perfectly well I'm safe, but I just get this little urge to step back out where there's more room to breathe. \n\nI try not to make a fuss, of course. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces related to not being able to easily get out if you need to?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's not so much about being trapped, exactly. I mean, I know the door isn't locked, usually. It's not like I think I'm a prisoner or anything silly like that. \n\nIt's really just the feeling of the walls being too close, you know? Just the... the smallness of it all. I know I could push the door open or press the button if I really had to, so it's not that I think I can't escape. I just... I just don't like the tight squeeze. \n\nI hope I'm not repeating myself too much. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea about it all. I suppose I do, a little bit. It's nothing major, really, just small adjustments so I don't hold anyone up or make a scene. Like... well, these days, I just take the stairs instead of the elevator. It's good exercise for a fellow my age anyway, right? \n\nAnd down at the museum where I volunteer, I usually pass on the archive jobs in the basement. Or, if I absolutely have to go into a tight spot and there's no way around it, I might... well, I might ask a friend to just wait nearby. Just in case. \n\nI know it sounds a bit foolish, a grown man needing a buddy to check a storage closet. I try not to be a bother about it, honestly. Does that sound like the sort of thing you're asking about? I hope I'm answering your questions properly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a sudden, intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes, with symptoms like a racing heart or shortness of breath, in these situations?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing quite that dramatic, I don't think. I mean... my heart might beat a little bit quicker just because I'm eager to step back out, but it doesn't race, and I certainly don't lose my breath over it. \n\nIt's really just a strong urge to turn around and leave, not a... well, not a big episode or anything. I don't fall to pieces. I hope that's okay? I know you have to ask these things to figure out what's going on, so I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers. Am I being helpful at all?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear or discomfort limited to being in enclosed spaces, or are there other situations that trigger similar feelings?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really think it's just the small spaces, honestly. I don't get that... that feeling anywhere else. \n\nI mean, I can be out in a crowd, or up high somewhere, or... or even flying on an airplane, and I'm right as rain. It's just those little windowless rooms and elevators that seem to bother me. \n\nI hope that's the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you all the pieces you need. I really don't think there's anything else that sets it off, but if you want me to think on it more, I certainly can.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of being trapped or the small size of the space itself that bothers you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more about feeling trapped. I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up, you know? It's perfectly safe. But... well, my mind just treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. \n\nIt's silly, isn't it? A man my age getting worked up over a door closing. I try to just brush it off so it doesn't bother anyone. Does that make sense to you, Doctor? I hope I'm explaining it right so it helps you figure this out.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that something terrible will happen to you when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I don't... I don't think I'm going to be hurt or anything like that. It's not like I'm worried the ceiling is going to cave in on me or the elevator cables will snap. \n\nIt's just that uncomfortable feeling, you know? Just wanting to be back out where there's a bit more room to breathe. I certainly don't let my imagination run away with me about terrible accidents or anything. I try to keep a level head about it. \n\nI hope that's a good thing? I mean, I figure if I don't have those awful thoughts, then maybe it's not such a big problem after all. Does that sound right to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack or some other intense reaction when you are in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. I... I don't think it's anything like that. A panic attack sounds so... well, so severe, doesn't it? I certainly don't go having those. I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I'd hate to be a bother to anyone, so I just try to be a good sport and push through it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I... I'd never do anything like that. Losing control, I mean. That would be terribly embarrassing, wouldn't it? \n\nI just get a little eager to step back out, that's all. I certainly don't feel like my body is going to do anything frightening or... or strange. I've always prided myself on keeping a stiff upper lip, you know. I just stand quietly and mind my own business until I can leave. \n\nI hope you don't think I'm the sort of fellow who goes to pieces over a small room? I try very hard to be sensible about it, Doctor, and just behave myself. Is that the sort of answer you were looking for? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in enclosed spaces, or is there something specific about those spaces that frightens you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's just the spaces themselves, I suppose. It's not that I think there's a monster in the closet or anything silly like that. \n\nI know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being... well, locked up. But my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's just that feeling of being shut in, you know? \n\nI try to remind myself that it's just a room and I'm perfectly safe. I'm not being foolish about it, am I? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it properly so I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Is it the lack of an easy way to exit that bothers you when you're in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose you could say that, yes. It's just that feeling of the door being closed and not being able to just step right back out if I wanted to. \n\nIt's not that I think I'm trapped forever, of course. I know perfectly well that people come and go from these places all day long without a hitch. I just prefer to have a clear path to the door, that's all. \n\nI try to just stand near the exit and not make a fuss about it. I don't want to seem like an old fool who can't handle a simple elevator ride. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear that being in an enclosed space will trigger some kind of intense anxiety episode?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. Nothing like that. I don't have... episodes or anything dramatic. I'm not the type to just fly off the handle or make a scene. \n\nIt's just a mild discomfort, really. A little flutter, maybe, but I just keep it to myself. I certainly wouldn't call it intense anxiety. I've seen people get all worked up over things, and I always think it's better to just keep your composure, don't you agree? \n\nI just try to be a good sport about it. I hope you don't think I'm one of those people who can't control their nerves. I'm doing my best to be helpful and give you the right answers, Doctor. Am I doing alright?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only about being in small or enclosed spaces, without any other specific concerns?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I believe so. It's really just the small spaces. I don't have any other peculiar worries or... or odd habits, if that's what you mean. \n\nI'm not afraid of heights or spiders or anything like that. I try to be very sensible about most things, you know? It's really just that one little quirk about being cooped up in a tight spot, and even then, I manage it as best I can. \n\nI hope that helps narrow things down for you. I'm trying to be as clear as I can so I don't confuse matters... is that the sort of answer you were looking for, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Is it the small size of the space itself that bothers you, or is it more about not being able to leave when you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more the space itself. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a cell or anything dreadful like that. \n\nBut my mind just... well, it treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's silly, isn't it? I know perfectly well I'm safe, but I just get this little urge to step back out where there's more room to breathe. \n\nI try not to make a fuss, of course. I just politely take the stairs if I can, or wait by the door. I hope that makes sense, Doctor. I'm not trying to be difficult, I just want to explain it right so you have what you need. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces related to not being able to easily get out if you need to?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's not so much about being trapped, exactly. I mean, I know the door isn't locked, usually. It's not like I think I'm a prisoner or anything silly like that. \n\nIt's really just the feeling of the walls being too close, you know? Just the... the smallness of it all. I know I could push the door open or press the button if I really had to, so it's not that I think I can't escape. I just... I just don't like the tight squeeze. \n\nI hope I'm not repeating myself too much. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right idea about it all. Does that answer your question, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having a panic attack when in these enclosed spaces, even if you've never actually had one?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. I... I... I wouldn't ever make a scene or lose my temper just because I'm in a small room. I've always prided myself on keeping my composure, even when I'm a bit uncomfortable. \n\nIt's just a little... a little jumpiness, I suppose you could call it. Just a strong preference for having some elbow room. I certainly wouldn't start shouting or crying or doing anything foolish. I just quietly excuse myself if it gets to be too much. \n\nI hope I'm being helpful, Doctor. I know some folks might have a harder time with it, and I don't want to make my little quirk sound worse than it is. I'm doing alright, aren't I?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I think... I think you might have asked me that just a moment ago, Doctor. But no, I really don't. 

Like I was saying, I've never been the type to panic. I've been around a long time, and you learn to just take a breath and deal with things. It's just a mild preference for open spaces, really. I don't think it's anything like that. A panic attack sounds so... well, so severe, doesn't it? I certainly don't go having those. I wouldn't want to make a scene or cause a fuss for anyone around me. \n\nI just get a little, you know, antsy. I try to keep it all under my hat and just wait quietly until the door opens again. I'd hate to be a bother to anyone, so I just try to be a good sport and push through it. \n\nI hope that makes sense? I mean, I figure I'm doing alright if I can just keep my composure, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your desire to leave enclosed spaces is because you're afraid of losing control or having some kind of frightening physical reaction while you're in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. It's just that feeling of the door being closed and not being able to just step right back out if I wanted to. \n\nIt's not that I think I'm trapped forever, of course. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only of being in enclosed spaces, or is there something specific about those spaces that frightens you?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's just the spaces themselves, I suppose. It's not that I think there's a monster in the closet or anything silly like that. \n\nI know a little storage room or, say, an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being... well, locked up. But my mind just sort of treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's just that feeling of being shut in, you know? \n\nI try to remind myself that it's just a room and I'm perfectly safe. I'm not being foolish about it, am I? I just want to make sure I'm explaining it properly so I'm not wasting your time.\n\nDoctor: Is it the lack of an easy way to exit that bothers you when you're in these enclosed spaces?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose you could say that, yes. It's just that feeling of the door being closed and not being able to just step right back out if I wanted to. \n\nIt's not that I think I'm trapped forever, of course. I know perfectly well that people come and go from these places all day long without a hitch. I just prefer to have a clear path to the door, that's all. \n\nI try to just stand near the exit and not make a fuss about it. I don't want to seem like an old fool who can't handle a simple elevator ride. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear that being in an enclosed space will trigger some kind of intense anxiety episode?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no. Nothing like that. I don't have... episodes or anything dramatic. I'm not the type to just fly off the handle or make a scene. \n\nIt's just a mild discomfort, really. A little flutter, maybe, but I just keep it to myself. I certainly wouldn't call it intense anxiety. I've seen people get all worked up over things, and I always think it's better to just keep your composure, don't you agree? \n\nI just try to be a good sport about it. I hope you don't think I'm one of those people who can't control their nerves. I'm doing my best to be helpful and give you the right answers, Doctor. Am I doing alright?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear only about being in small or enclosed spaces, without any other specific concerns?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I believe so. It's really just the small spaces. I don't have any other peculiar worries or... or odd habits, if that's what you mean. \n\nI'm not afraid of heights or spiders or anything like that. I try to be very sensible about most things, you know? It's really just that one little quirk about being cooped up in a tight spot, and even then, I manage it as best I can. \n\nI hope that helps narrow things down for you. I'm trying to be as clear as I can so I don't confuse matters... is that the sort of answer you were looking for, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Is it the small size of the space itself that bothers you, or is it more about not being able to leave when you want to?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's a bit of both, but maybe more the space itself. I mean, I know a little storage room or an elevator at the museum isn't the same as being locked up in a cell or anything dreadful like that. \n\nBut my mind just... well, it treats it like trouble the minute I see there's no window and the door shuts tight. It's silly, isn't it? I know perfectly well I'm safe, but I just get this little urge to step back out where there's more room to breathe. \n\nI try not to make a fuss, of course. I just politely take the stairs if I can, or wait by the door. I hope that makes sense, Doctor. I'm not trying to be difficult, I just want to explain it right so you have what you need. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of enclosed spaces related to not being able to easily get out if you need to?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose it's not so much about being trapped, exactly. I mean, I know the door isn't locked, usually. It's not like I think I'm a prisoner or anything silly like that. \n\nIt's really just the feeling of the walls being too close, you know? Just the... the smallness of it all. I know I could push the door open or press the button if I really had to, so it's not that I think I can't escape. I just... I don't think I've ever had a real panic attack, and I certainly don't go around worrying that I might suddenly have one. I try to keep a pretty level head, you know? \n\nIt's really just a mild discomfort with the tight space itself, just wanting a bit more room. I certainly don't lose control or anything dramatic like that. I just feel a little uneasy until I'm back out in the open. \n\nI hope that's alright to say... I mean, I wouldn't want you to think I'm wasting your time with something so small, but I really don't have those kinds of severe worries. Am I giving you the right sort of details, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you fear that being in an enclosed space might lead to a loss of control over your emotions or behavior?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, nothing like that. I... I wouldn't ever make a scene or lose my temper just because I'm in a small room. I've always prided myself on keeping my composure, even when I'm a bit uncomfortable. \n\nIt's just a little... a little jumpiness, I suppose you could call it. Just a strong preference for having some elbow room. I certainly wouldn't start shouting or crying or doing anything foolish. I just quietly excuse myself if it gets to be too much. \n\nI hope I'm being helpful, Doctor. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no... no, I don't think so. There's nothing... nothing hiding in the dark or anything like that, if that's what you mean. It really is just the... well, the tightness of it all. Just the size. 

I'm not afraid the ceiling is going to cave in or that there's something dangerous in there with me. I know it's perfectly safe, just an ordinary room. It's just my own silly preference for a bit of breathing room, really. Nothing more to it than that.
